People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I mean, I tell myself I’m just being thorough, but really I keep going back over the breakers, the labels, torque marks, the photos… and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site. It calms me down a bit, but it’s gotten to where simple installs take way longer than they should. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work’s been getting harder than it should. I keep going back over jobs—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, pictures I took. Sometimes I even drive back to a site to make sure. Simple installs end up taking way too long, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking these things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s like I can’t leave it alone. I’ll check the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took… and sometimes I still end up going back to the site. It settles me for a bit, I guess, but it’s gotten to where the job takes way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. Even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed something—like a loose neutral, or something that could start a fire. I try to push it away, but it kind of follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a strict order, but I end up going back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—again and again until it feels okay enough to leave. Sometimes it still doesn’t, and I’ll drive back. It makes simple jobs take way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what it feels like. Like if I don’t go back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, all that, something could be wrong and I’d be the one who missed it. So I keep checking, or looking at the photos, and sometimes I even drive back. I know it makes the job take way too long… people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed your checks?\n\nPatient: For a little while, yeah. Once I’ve gone back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… it settles down some. But it doesn’t always last. Sometimes I still feel like I should go back, and that’s when a simple job ends up taking way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things over and over if someone else, like a colleague, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work’s been getting harder than it should. I keep going back over jobs—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, pictures I took. Sometimes I even drive back to a site to make sure. Simple installs end up taking way too long, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking these things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s like I can’t leave it alone. I’ll check the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took… and sometimes I still end up going back to the site. It settles me for a bit, I guess, but it’s gotten to where the job takes way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. Even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed something—like a loose neutral, or something that could start a fire. I try to push it away, but it kind of follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a strict order, but I end up going back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—again and again until it feels okay enough to leave. Sometimes it still doesn’t, and I’ll drive back. It makes simple jobs take way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what it feels like. Like if I don’t go back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, all that, something could be wrong and I’d be the one who missed it. So I keep checking, or looking at the photos, and sometimes I even drive back. I know it makes the job take way too long… people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed your checks?\n\nPatient: For a little while, yeah. Once I’ve gone back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… it settles down some. But it doesn’t always last. Sometimes I still feel like I should go back, and that’s when a simple job ends up taking way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I tell myself I’m just being thorough, but really I keep going back over the breakers, the labels, torque marks, the photos… and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site. It calms me down a bit, but it’s gotten to where simple installs take way longer than they should. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things over and over if someone else, like a colleague, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. Even if someone else looked it over, I’d still feel like I needed to see it myself. I’d end up checking the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… maybe more than once. It’s hard for me to leave it alone, and that’s where the job starts taking way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over is because you're afraid of making a mistake, or is it because you just like things done a certain way?\n\nPatient: More afraid of making a mistake, I think. It’s not really that I like things a certain way… I just keep going back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, all of it. Sometimes even driving back to a site. It gets out of hand, and then a simple job takes way too long.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with colleagues or friends due to the time it takes you to complete your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, with colleagues, especially the apprentices, it’s getting harder because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks. It throws the schedule off, and I can tell clients don’t always love that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I’ve been skipping stuff after work more, because I’m still reviewing a job or thinking I might need to go back. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work’s been getting harder than it should. I keep going back over jobs—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, pictures I took. Sometimes I even drive back to a site to make sure. Simple installs end up taking way too long, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking these things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s like I can’t leave it alone. I’ll check the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took… and sometimes I still end up going back to the site. It settles me for a bit, I guess, but it’s gotten to where the job takes way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. Even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed something—like a loose neutral, or something that could start a fire. I try to push it away, but it kind of follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a strict order, but I end up going back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—again and again until it feels okay enough to leave. Sometimes it still doesn’t, and I’ll drive back. It makes simple jobs take way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what it feels like. Like if I don’t go back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, all that, something could be wrong and I’d be the one who missed it. So I keep checking, or looking at the photos, and sometimes I even drive back. I know it makes the job take way too long… people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed your checks?\n\nPatient: For a little while, yeah. Once I’ve gone back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… it settles down some. But it doesn’t always last. Sometimes I still feel like I should go back, and that’s when a simple job ends up taking way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I tell myself I’m just being thorough, but really I keep going back over the breakers, the labels, torque marks, the photos… and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site. It calms me down a bit, but it’s gotten to where simple installs take way longer than they should. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things over and over if someone else, like a colleague, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. Even if someone else looked it over, I’d still feel like I needed to see it myself. I’d end up checking the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… maybe more than once. It’s hard for me to leave it alone, and that’s where the job starts taking way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over is because you're afraid of making a mistake, or is it because you just like things done a certain way?\n\nPatient: More afraid of making a mistake, I think. It’s not really that I like things a certain way… I just keep going back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, all of it. Sometimes even driving back to a site. It gets out of hand, and then a simple job takes way too long.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with colleagues or friends due to the time it takes you to complete your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, with colleagues, especially the apprentices, it’s getting harder because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks. It throws the schedule off, and I can tell clients don’t always love that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I’ve been skipping stuff after work more, because I’m still reviewing a job or thinking I might need to go back. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to check things over and over is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t really like admitting that, but my schedule’s been slipping. Jobs that should be one visit turn into callbacks or extra checks, and that eats up time with the apprentices and makes clients less confident, I think.\n\nAnd after work, I’m… not doing as much. I skip things I used to enjoy because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or worrying I need to go back. So, yeah, it’s affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not more than anybody else, I don’t think. I worry about work stuff mostly. The other things—health, money, relationships—I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about them for no reason.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

The other things—health, money, relationships—I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about them for no reason.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these thoughts and urges to check things are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"Even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought keeps barging in on me that maybe I missed one loose neutral or left something that could start a fire, and I try to shake it off but it follows me all the way home.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess… not like a voice or anything outside.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work’s been getting harder than it should. I keep going back over jobs—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, pictures I took. Sometimes I even drive back to a site to make sure. Simple installs end up taking way too long, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking these things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s like I can’t leave it alone. I’ll check the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took… and sometimes I still end up going back to the site. It settles me for a bit, I guess, but it’s gotten to where the job takes way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. Even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed something—like a loose neutral, or something that could start a fire. I try to push it away, but it kind of follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a strict order, but I end up going back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—again and again until it feels okay enough to leave. Sometimes it still doesn’t, and I’ll drive back. It makes simple jobs take way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what it feels like. Like if I don’t go back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, all that, something could be wrong and I’d be the one who missed it. So I keep checking, or looking at the photos, and sometimes I even drive back. I know it makes the job take way too long… people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed your checks?\n\nPatient: For a little while, yeah. Once I’ve gone back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… it settles down some. But it doesn’t always last. Sometimes I still feel like I should go back, and that’s when a simple job ends up taking way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I tell myself I’m just being thorough, but really I keep going back over the breakers, the labels, torque marks, the photos… and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site. It calms me down a bit, but it’s gotten to where simple installs take way longer than they should. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things over and over if someone else, like a colleague, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. Even if someone else looked it over, I’d still feel like I needed to see it myself. I’d end up checking the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… maybe more than once. It’s hard for me to leave it alone, and that’s where the job starts taking way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over is because you're afraid of making a mistake, or is it because you just like things done a certain way?\n\nPatient: More afraid of making a mistake, I think. It’s not really that I like things a certain way… I just keep going back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, all of it. Sometimes even driving back to a site. It gets out of hand, and then a simple job takes way too long.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with colleagues or friends due to the time it takes you to complete your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, with colleagues, especially the apprentices, it’s getting harder because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks. It throws the schedule off, and I can tell clients don’t always love that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I’ve been skipping stuff after work more, because I’m still reviewing a job or thinking I might need to go back. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to check things over and over is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t really like admitting that, but my schedule’s been slipping. Jobs that should be one visit turn into callbacks or extra checks, and that eats up time with the apprentices and makes clients less confident, I think.\n\nAnd after work, I’m… not doing as much. I skip things I used to enjoy because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or worrying I need to go back. So, yeah, it’s affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not more than anybody else, I don’t think. I worry about work stuff mostly. The other things—health, money, relationships—I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about them for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and urges to check things are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess… not like a voice or anything outside.\n\nIt’s more like after I’ve already checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought just barges back in—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, what if something could start a fire. I try to shake it off, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

So I keep checking, or looking at the photos, and sometimes I even drive back. I know it makes the job take way too long… people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed your checks?\n\nPatient: For a little while, yeah. Once I’ve gone back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… it settles down some. But it doesn’t always last. Sometimes I still feel like I should go back, and that’s when a simple job ends up taking way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I tell myself I’m just being thorough, but really I keep going back over the breakers, the labels, torque marks, the photos… and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site. It calms me down a bit, but it’s gotten to where simple installs take way longer than they should. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things over and over if someone else, like a colleague, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. Even if someone else looked it over, I’d still feel like I needed to see it myself. I’d end up checking the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… maybe more than once. It’s hard for me to leave it alone, and that’s where the job starts taking way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over is because you're afraid of making a mistake, or is it because you just like things done a certain way?\n\nPatient: More afraid of making a mistake, I think. It’s not really that I like things a certain way… I just keep going back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, all of it. Sometimes even driving back to a site. It gets out of hand, and then a simple job takes way too long.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with colleagues or friends due to the time it takes you to complete your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, with colleagues, especially the apprentices, it’s getting harder because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks. It throws the schedule off, and I can tell clients don’t always love that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I’ve been skipping stuff after work more, because I’m still reviewing a job or thinking I might need to go back. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to check things over and over is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t really like admitting that, but my schedule’s been slipping. Jobs that should be one visit turn into callbacks or extra checks, and that eats up time with the apprentices and makes clients less confident, I think.\n\nAnd after work, I’m… not doing as much. I skip things I used to enjoy because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or worrying I need to go back. So, yeah, it’s affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not more than anybody else, I don’t think. I worry about work stuff mostly. The other things—health, money, relationships—I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about them for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and urges to check things are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess… not like a voice or anything outside.\n\nIt’s more like after I’ve already checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought just barges back in—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, what if something could start a fire. I try to shake it off, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking habits are interfering with your ability to enjoy your free time or pursue hobbies outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really get into much after work lately.\n\nEven when I’m home, I’m still going back over a job in my head, wondering if I need to drive back and check something. So I end up skipping things I used to do. And during the day, it’s messing with the schedule too—jobs that should be done in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks, and that’s starting to affect clients and the apprentices.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… work’s been getting harder than it should. I keep going back over jobs—checking breakers, labels, torque marks, pictures I took. Sometimes I even drive back to a site to make sure. Simple installs end up taking way too long, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking these things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s like I can’t leave it alone. I’ll check the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took… and sometimes I still end up going back to the site. It settles me for a bit, I guess, but it’s gotten to where the job takes way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. Even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed something—like a loose neutral, or something that could start a fire. I try to push it away, but it kind of follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a strict order, but I end up going back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—again and again until it feels okay enough to leave. Sometimes it still doesn’t, and I’ll drive back. It makes simple jobs take way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what it feels like. Like if I don’t go back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, all that, something could be wrong and I’d be the one who missed it. So I keep checking, or looking at the photos, and sometimes I even drive back. I know it makes the job take way too long… people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed your checks?\n\nPatient: For a little while, yeah. Once I’ve gone back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… it settles down some. But it doesn’t always last. Sometimes I still feel like I should go back, and that’s when a simple job ends up taking way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I tell myself I’m just being thorough, but really I keep going back over the breakers, the labels, torque marks, the photos… and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site. It calms me down a bit, but it’s gotten to where simple installs take way longer than they should. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things over and over if someone else, like a colleague, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. Even if someone else looked it over, I’d still feel like I needed to see it myself. I’d end up checking the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… maybe more than once. It’s hard for me to leave it alone, and that’s where the job starts taking way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over is because you're afraid of making a mistake, or is it because you just like things done a certain way?\n\nPatient: More afraid of making a mistake, I think. It’s not really that I like things a certain way… I just keep going back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, all of it. Sometimes even driving back to a site. It gets out of hand, and then a simple job takes way too long.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with colleagues or friends due to the time it takes you to complete your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, with colleagues, especially the apprentices, it’s getting harder because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks. It throws the schedule off, and I can tell clients don’t always love that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I’ve been skipping stuff after work more, because I’m still reviewing a job or thinking I might need to go back. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to check things over and over is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t really like admitting that, but my schedule’s been slipping. Jobs that should be one visit turn into callbacks or extra checks, and that eats up time with the apprentices and makes clients less confident, I think.\n\nAnd after work, I’m… not doing as much. I skip things I used to enjoy because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or worrying I need to go back. So, yeah, it’s affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not more than anybody else, I don’t think. I worry about work stuff mostly. The other things—health, money, relationships—I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about them for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and urges to check things are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess… not like a voice or anything outside.\n\nIt’s more like after I’ve already checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought just barges back in—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, what if something could start a fire. I try to shake it off, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking habits are interfering with your ability to enjoy your free time or pursue hobbies outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really get into much after work lately.\n\nEven when I’m home, I’m still going back over a job in my head, wondering if I need to drive back and check something. So I end up skipping things I used to do. And during the day, it’s messing with the schedule too—jobs that should be done in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks, and that’s starting to affect clients and the apprentices.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these repeating thoughts or feel the need to check things when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s tied to work.\n\nWhen I’m not on a job, I still get stuck going back over it in my head… breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll check the photos again, or even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. It’s not really random stuff at home—it’s the work checks that follow me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like you're able to function normally and without these checking thoughts or behaviors interfering?\n\nPatient: I guess some things are still okay, but work and after work are where it really gets in the way.\n\nMy schedule’s slipping because I turn jobs that should be one visit into extra checks or callbacks. It’s… starting to affect how clients see me, and it takes time away from the apprentices too. And when I’m home, I skip stuff I used to enjoy because I’m still going over the job in my head or wondering if I need to go back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It makes simple jobs take way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what it feels like. Like if I don’t go back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, all that, something could be wrong and I’d be the one who missed it. So I keep checking, or looking at the photos, and sometimes I even drive back. I know it makes the job take way too long… people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed your checks?\n\nPatient: For a little while, yeah. Once I’ve gone back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… it settles down some. But it doesn’t always last. Sometimes I still feel like I should go back, and that’s when a simple job ends up taking way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I tell myself I’m just being thorough, but really I keep going back over the breakers, the labels, torque marks, the photos… and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site. It calms me down a bit, but it’s gotten to where simple installs take way longer than they should. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things over and over if someone else, like a colleague, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. Even if someone else looked it over, I’d still feel like I needed to see it myself. I’d end up checking the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… maybe more than once. It’s hard for me to leave it alone, and that’s where the job starts taking way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over is because you're afraid of making a mistake, or is it because you just like things done a certain way?\n\nPatient: More afraid of making a mistake, I think. It’s not really that I like things a certain way… I just keep going back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, all of it. Sometimes even driving back to a site. It gets out of hand, and then a simple job takes way too long.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with colleagues or friends due to the time it takes you to complete your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, with colleagues, especially the apprentices, it’s getting harder because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks. It throws the schedule off, and I can tell clients don’t always love that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I’ve been skipping stuff after work more, because I’m still reviewing a job or thinking I might need to go back. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to check things over and over is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t really like admitting that, but my schedule’s been slipping. Jobs that should be one visit turn into callbacks or extra checks, and that eats up time with the apprentices and makes clients less confident, I think.\n\nAnd after work, I’m… not doing as much. I skip things I used to enjoy because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or worrying I need to go back. So, yeah, it’s affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not more than anybody else, I don’t think. I worry about work stuff mostly. The other things—health, money, relationships—I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about them for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and urges to check things are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess… not like a voice or anything outside.\n\nIt’s more like after I’ve already checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought just barges back in—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, what if something could start a fire. I try to shake it off, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking habits are interfering with your ability to enjoy your free time or pursue hobbies outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really get into much after work lately.\n\nEven when I’m home, I’m still going back over a job in my head, wondering if I need to drive back and check something. So I end up skipping things I used to do. And during the day, it’s messing with the schedule too—jobs that should be done in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks, and that’s starting to affect clients and the apprentices.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these repeating thoughts or feel the need to check things when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s tied to work.\n\nWhen I’m not on a job, I still get stuck going back over it in my head… breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll check the photos again, or even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. It’s not really random stuff at home—it’s the work checks that follow me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like you're able to function normally and without these checking thoughts or behaviors interfering?\n\nPatient: I guess some things are still okay, but work and after work are where it really gets in the way.\n\nMy schedule’s slipping because I turn jobs that should be one visit into extra checks or callbacks. It’s… starting to affect how clients see me, and it takes time away from the apprentices too. And when I’m home, I skip stuff I used to enjoy because I’m still going over the job in my head or wondering if I need to go back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you're unable to check things the way you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I get pretty wound up if I can’t check it the way I want.\n\nIt’s like I can’t leave it alone—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos. I keep thinking I missed something, and then I end up going back over it again, sometimes even driving back. I know it makes the job take way longer than it should… and people are noticing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Simple installs end up taking way too long, and people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're checking these things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s like I can’t leave it alone. I’ll check the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took… and sometimes I still end up going back to the site. It settles me for a bit, I guess, but it’s gotten to where the job takes way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. Even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed something—like a loose neutral, or something that could start a fire. I try to push it away, but it kind of follows me home.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t know if it’s always a strict order, but I end up going back through the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos—again and again until it feels okay enough to leave. Sometimes it still doesn’t, and I’ll drive back. It makes simple jobs take way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what it feels like. Like if I don’t go back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, all that, something could be wrong and I’d be the one who missed it. So I keep checking, or looking at the photos, and sometimes I even drive back. I know it makes the job take way too long… people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've completed your checks?\n\nPatient: For a little while, yeah. Once I’ve gone back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… it settles down some. But it doesn’t always last. Sometimes I still feel like I should go back, and that’s when a simple job ends up taking way too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I tell myself I’m just being thorough, but really I keep going back over the breakers, the labels, torque marks, the photos… and sometimes I’ll even drive back to the site. It calms me down a bit, but it’s gotten to where simple installs take way longer than they should. People are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you check these things, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s both, but… mostly I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. Like with the breakers or labels or torque marks. I’ll check the photos too, and sometimes I’ve even gone back to the site.\n\nIt’s not always one specific thing I can name. It just feels like if I don’t check, something’s going to be wrong, and I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check these things over and over if someone else, like a colleague, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. Even if someone else looked it over, I’d still feel like I needed to see it myself. I’d end up checking the breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos… maybe more than once. It’s hard for me to leave it alone, and that’s where the job starts taking way longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things over and over is because you're afraid of making a mistake, or is it because you just like things done a certain way?\n\nPatient: More afraid of making a mistake, I think. It’s not really that I like things a certain way… I just keep going back over the breakers, labels, torque marks, photos, all of it. Sometimes even driving back to a site. It gets out of hand, and then a simple job takes way too long.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain relationships with colleagues or friends due to the time it takes you to complete your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, with colleagues, especially the apprentices, it’s getting harder because jobs I should wrap up in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks. It throws the schedule off, and I can tell clients don’t always love that.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I’ve been skipping stuff after work more, because I’m still reviewing a job or thinking I might need to go back. So, yeah, it’s starting to get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to check things over and over is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I don’t really like admitting that, but my schedule’s been slipping. Jobs that should be one visit turn into callbacks or extra checks, and that eats up time with the apprentices and makes clients less confident, I think.\n\nAnd after work, I’m… not doing as much. I skip things I used to enjoy because I’m still reviewing a job in my head or worrying I need to go back. So, yeah, it’s affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like your health, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, not more than anybody else, I don’t think. I worry about work stuff mostly. The other things—health, money, relationships—I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying about them for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and urges to check things are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, I guess… not like a voice or anything outside.\n\nIt’s more like after I’ve already checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought just barges back in—like, what if I missed one loose neutral, what if something could start a fire. I try to shake it off, but it can follow me all the way home.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking habits are interfering with your ability to enjoy your free time or pursue hobbies outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I don’t really get into much after work lately.\n\nEven when I’m home, I’m still going back over a job in my head, wondering if I need to drive back and check something. So I end up skipping things I used to do. And during the day, it’s messing with the schedule too—jobs that should be done in one visit turn into extra checks or callbacks, and that’s starting to affect clients and the apprentices.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these repeating thoughts or feel the need to check things when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s tied to work.\n\nWhen I’m not on a job, I still get stuck going back over it in my head… breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took. Sometimes I’ll check the photos again, or even drive back to the site if I can’t let it go. It’s not really random stuff at home—it’s the work checks that follow me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like you're able to function normally and without these checking thoughts or behaviors interfering?\n\nPatient: I guess some things are still okay, but work and after work are where it really gets in the way.\n\nMy schedule’s slipping because I turn jobs that should be one visit into extra checks or callbacks. It’s… starting to affect how clients see me, and it takes time away from the apprentices too. And when I’m home, I skip stuff I used to enjoy because I’m still going over the job in my head or wondering if I need to go back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel distressed when you're unable to check things the way you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I get pretty wound up if I can’t check it the way I want.\n\nIt’s like I can’t leave it alone—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos. I keep thinking I missed something, and then I end up going back over it again, sometimes even driving back. I know it makes the job take way longer than it should… and people are noticing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your life, besides work and free time, where you feel like these checking thoughts or behaviors are causing problems?\n\nPatient: Mostly the business side, I guess. My schedule’s getting messed up because I don’t finish when I should… jobs turn into extra checks or callbacks.\n\nAnd it’s starting to affect clients trusting me, I think. Plus I’m not giving the apprentices the time I should. Outside of that… I’ve been skipping things after work I used to do, because I’m still stuck thinking about the job or whether I need to go back."
